1 When he went into the house of one of the rulers of the Pharisees on a Sabbath to eat bread⌘, they were watching him.◇ 2 Behold, a certain man who had dropsy was in front of him.◇ 3 Jesus, answering, spoke to the lawyers and Pharisees, saying, “Is it lawful to heal on the Sabbath?” 4 But they were silent. He took him, and healed him, and let him go. 5 He answered them, “Which of you, if your son or an ox fell into a well, wouldn’t immediately pull him out on a Sabbath day?” 6 They couldn’t answer him regarding these things. 7 He spoke a parable to those who were invited, when he noticed how they chose the best seats, and said to them,◇ 8 “When you are invited by anyone to a wedding feast, don’t sit in the best seat, since perhaps someone more honorable than you might be invited by him,‖◇ 9 and he who invited both of you would come and tell you, ‘Make room for this person.’ Then you would begin, with shame, to take the lowest place. 10 But when you are invited, go and sit in the lowest place, so that when he who invited you comes, he may tell you, ‘Friend, move up higher.’ Then you will be honored in the presence of all who sit at the table with you.◇ 11 For everyone who exalts himself will be humbled, and whoever humbles himself will be exalted.”‖‖◇◇ 12 He also said to the one who had invited him, “When you make a dinner or a supper, don’t call your friends, nor your brothers, nor your kinsmen, nor rich neighbors, or perhaps they might also return the favor, and pay you back.◇ 13 But when you make a feast, ask the poor, the maimed, the lame, or the blind; 14 and you will be blessed⌘, because they don’t have the resources to repay you. For you will be repaid in the resurrection⌘ of the righteous.”◇ 15 When one of those who sat at the table with him heard these things, he said to him, “Blessed⌘ is he who will feast in God⌘’s Kingdom!”◇ 16 But he said to him, “A certain man made a great supper, and he invited many people.◇◇ 17 He sent out his servant at supper time to tell those who were invited, ‘Come, for everything is ready now.’◇ 18 They all as one began to make excuses. “The first said to him, ‘I have bought a field, and I must go and see it. Please have me excused.’◇ 19 “Another said, ‘I have bought five yoke of oxen, and I must go try them out. Please have me excused.’ 20 “Another said, ‘I have married a wife, and therefore I can’t come.’ 21 “That servant came, and told his lord⌘ these things. Then the master of the house, being angry, said to his servant, ‘Go out quickly into the streets and lanes of the city, and bring in the poor, maimed, blind, and lame.’ 22 “The servant said, ‘Lord⌘, it is done as you commanded, and there is still room.’ 23 “The lord⌘ said to the servant, ‘Go out into the highways and hedges, and compel them to come in, that my house may be filled.◇◇ 24 For I tell you that none of those men who were invited will taste of my supper. For many are called, but few are chosen.’”◇ 25 Now great multitudes were going with him. He turned and said to them,◇ 26 “If anyone comes to me, and doesn’t disregard his own father⌘, mother, wife, children, brothers, and sisters, yes, and his own life⌘ also, he can’t be my disciple.‖‖◇ 27 Whoever doesn’t bear his own cross and come after me, can’t be my disciple.‖◇◇ 28 For which of you, desiring to build a tower, doesn’t first sit down and count the cost, to see if he has enough to complete it?‖‖◇ 29 Or perhaps, when he has laid a foundation and isn’t able to finish, everyone who sees begins to mock him, 30 saying, ‘This man began to build and wasn’t able to finish.’ 31 Or what king, as he goes to encounter another king in war, will not sit down first and consider whether he is able with ten thousand to meet him who comes against him with twenty thousand? 32 Or else, while the other is yet a great way⌘ off, he sends an envoy and asks for conditions of peace⌘.◇ 33 So therefore, whoever of you who doesn’t renounce all that he has, he can’t be my disciple.‖◇ 34 “Salt is good, but if the salt becomes flat and tasteless, with what do you season it?◇ 35 It is fit neither for the soil nor for the manure pile. It is thrown out. He who has ears to hear, let him hear.”

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On 'Zacharias, of the course of Abia... his wife was of the daughters of Aaron' (Lk 1:5), Cyril reads the doubled priestly pedigree (Zacharias by course + Elizabeth by lineage) as the canonical anchor of the New-Testament-emerging-from-the-Old-Testament-priesthood: the forerunner of the Lord is born to a fully-Aaronic family, the last priestly-house representative whose voice will yield to the One greater than himself. Tewahedo liturgical theology cites Lk 1:5 as the canonical anchor for the Aaronic origin of the Christian priesthood: the same priestly succession that Zacharias represented continues through Christ's apostles into the Tewahedo episcopate.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On Gabriel's 'Fear not, Zacharias: for thy prayer is heard' (Lk 1:13), Cyril reads the angelic 'fear not' as the canonical pattern of every later divine annunciation: the angel speaks reassurance before announcement, because divine presence is overwhelming even when its content is good news. The same 'fear not' will be spoken to Mary (Lk 1:30), to the shepherds (Lk 2:10), and by the risen Christ to the women (Mt 28:10). Tewahedo angelology cites Lk 1:13 as the canonical anchor of the angelic-greeting-formula preserved in the Mäshafä Sǝʾatat (Book of Hours).

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

Cyril reads the angel's 'Hail, thou that art highly favoured' as the scriptural witness that Mary is uniquely graced — not merely a vessel selected at random but a creature on whom the eternal counsel of God has already rested. The greeting is itself an anticipation of the Incarnation: the angel speaks to her as already enclosing the favour that is about to descend in her womb. Tewahedo Marian theology cites this verse as one of its earliest patristic anchors of the Theotokos confession.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On Gabriel's 'The Holy Ghost shall come upon thee, and the power of the Highest shall overshadow thee: therefore also that holy thing which shall be born of thee shall be called the Son of God' (Lk 1:35), Cyril reads the doubled-pneumatology (Spirit-comes-upon + Highest-overshadows) as the canonical Lukan anchor of the Incarnation: not Adoption (the Spirit acts at conception, not at baptism), not Apollinarianism (the holy thing born IS the Son of God in his humanity), not Nestorianism (one subject is named, not two). The Tewahedo Theotokos doctrine cites Lk 1:35 as the canonical Lukan doubled-anchor with Mt 1:20 — the Spirit's-overshadowing makes Mary the bearer of God-in-flesh, not merely the bearer of a man.

◇ Athanasius of Alexandria (350). Athanasius of Alexandria Epistulae ad Serapionem 1.6 (c. 350 AD)

'And the angel answered and said unto her, The Holy Ghost shall come upon thee, and the power of the Highest shall overshadow thee: therefore also that holy thing which shall be born of thee shall be called the Son of God' — Athanasius marks the ANNUNCIATION as the PRINCIPAL TRINITARIAN-INCARNATIONAL-MOMENT: the Spirit (Pneuma Hagion) comes-upon the Virgin to prepare-and-sanctify her flesh as worthy-temple; the dynamis tou Hypsistou (power of the Highest) is the divine-Logos who is conceived-in-her; the Hypsistos (the Highest = the Father) is the sending-source. All three Persons are active in the single Annunciation-event. The episkiasei (overshadow) verb echoes the Shekinah-cloud overshadowing the tabernacle (Exo 40:34-38 LXX) — Mary becomes the new tabernacle of the Word's-glorious-presence. Tewahedo Marian-theology (Kidana Mahrat, the Covenant of Mercy) rests on this Annunciation-Trinitarian-overshadowing-Theotokos-ecclesia anchor.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On Mary's 'Behold the handmaid of the Lord; be it unto me according to thy word' (Lk 1:38), Cyril reads the fiat-mihi as the canonical anchor of the doctrine that the Incarnation requires human consent — a free-and-deliberate Yes from the side of Mary, not coerced. The Virgin's obedient response reverses Eve's refusal (Tertullian's typology, received by Cyril): Eve disbelieved and brought death, Mary believed and brought life. Tewahedo Marian piety cites Lk 1:38 as the canonical anchor of the doctrine that Mary is the New Eve whose deliberate Yes restores the human race to filial obedience — preserved in the Wǝddase Maryam refrain 'Behold, the handmaid of the Lord.'

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On Elizabeth's Spirit-inspired exclamation 'Blessed art thou among women, and blessed is the fruit of thy womb' (Lk 1:42), Cyril reads the doubled-beatitude (Mary blessed + the Fruit blessed) as the canonical Old-Testament-prophetic-Spirit-utterance preserved in the Lukan canticle structure: Elizabeth is the FIRST New-Testament prophetess, anticipating the broader Pentecost outpouring (Acts 2:18). Tewahedo Marian liturgy cites Lk 1:42 as the canonical anchor of the Ave-Maria pattern preserved in the Wǝddase Maryam and the Salam (Greeting) prayers; the Greeting (sälam) of Mary is the first New-Testament Marian title.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

The Magnificat — 'My soul doth magnify the Lord' — is for Cyril the first prophetic hymn of the New Covenant, spoken by the very mouth that bears the Word. Mary magnifies not by adding anything to God but by uniquely participating in his self-giving: she 'magnifies' the Lord precisely as the bearer of his enfleshment. Cyril reads the song as the OT prophetic genre — the canticles of Hannah, Miriam, Deborah — now fulfilled and surpassed in the Mother of the incarnate Word.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

Zacharias's tongue is loosed at the moment he writes 'His name is John,' and Cyril hears in the restoration the canonical sign that speech is given by faith and withheld by unbelief. The priestly silence imposed in the Holy Place is broken when the prophet consents to the angelic message; the canticle Benedictus follows immediately. Tewahedo lectionary preserves Zacharias's release as the OT-priestly-to-NT-prophetic hinge of the Lukan infancy.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On Zacharias's Benedictus phrase 'and hath raised up an horn of salvation for us in the house of his servant David' (Lk 1:69), Cyril reads the horn-of-salvation image as the canonical Davidic-messianic anchor: the horn signifies the strength of royal power, the house of David situates the Messiah in the legitimate covenantal lineage. The Benedictus is the second canonical canticle of Luke (after the Magnificat); Zacharias as priest prophesies the Messiah's Davidic descent under the influence of the Spirit. Tewahedo morning-office cites the Benedictus (with the Magnificat and Nunc Dimittis) as the canonical Lukan triad of Spirit-inspired infancy-canticles preserved in the Mäwasǝʾt (Antiphonary).

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

'No room in the inn' — Cyril hears the verse as the canonical sign that the Word enters the world rejected from the start, foreshadowing the Cross's final rejection. The manger is itself a eucharistic image: the food-trough of beasts becomes the resting-place of the Bread that comes down from heaven. Tewahedo Nativity hymnody draws directly on this Cyrillian reading of the manger as proto-altar.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On the angels' 'Glory to God in the highest, and on earth peace, good will toward men' (Lk 2:14), Cyril reads the angelic-hymn as the canonical Lukan anchor of the eucharistic Gloria: heaven and earth united in the act of praising God for the Incarnation. The doubled-line structure (heavenly glory + earthly peace) preserves the cosmic scope of the redemption: the Word's becoming-flesh reconciles the two realms. Tewahedo Mass cites Lk 2:14 as the canonical liturgical anchor for the 'Gloria in excelsis' (Sǝbḥat lä-ʿǝgziʾabḥer) preserved at the opening of every Anaphora; the Christmas Day proper extends the same canticle.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On Christ's circumcision 'when eight days were accomplished for the circumcising of the child' (Lk 2:21), Cyril reads the eighth-day circumcision as the canonical Lukan anchor that Christ deliberately submits to the Mosaic law (Gal 4:4 — 'made under the law'), preserving the Abrahamic covenant-sign in his own flesh. The naming 'Jesus' at the moment of circumcision links the covenant-sign to the salvation-name. Tewahedo theology — UNIQUELY among major Christian communions still practising bodily circumcision per Jub 15:14-25 — cites Lk 2:21 as the canonical NT anchor for the doctrine that Christ's circumcision honors the covenant-sign Christians continue to observe alongside the heart-circumcision (Deu 30:6).

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

Simeon's 'Nunc dimittis' is for Cyril the canonical confession that the seeing of the infant Christ IS the seeing of salvation: the eyes that behold the babe have beheld the salvation God prepared 'before the face of all peoples.' Cyril notes that Simeon does not say 'I have seen one who will save' but 'mine eyes have seen thy salvation' — the infant is the salvation, not merely its instrument. Tewahedo Compline preserves Nunc Dimittis as one of its earliest canonical-hymn anchors.

◇ Topic. Topics. This verse appears under: MARY. [Reviewer: pick one topic (typically the first or most theologically loaded), write a 2–3 sentence thematic note, and discard the rest.]

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On Simeon's prophecy to Mary 'yea, a sword shall pierce through thy own soul also' (Lk 2:35), Cyril reads the sword-image as the canonical Lukan anchor of the Marian compassion-at-the-Cross — preserved more fully in Jn 19:25 ('there stood by the cross of Jesus his mother'). The sword is not Mary's physical death but the maternal-soul-piercing of watching her Son crucified. Tewahedo Marian piety cites Lk 2:35 as the canonical Old-Testament-prophetic anchor that Mary's joys are inseparable from her sorrows; the seven-swords iconography preserved in the Tewahedo Wǝddase Maryam draws together the doxology of the Virgin with her maternal-passion compassion.

◇ Topic. Topics. This verse appears under: CHILDREN, JESUS, JESUS. [Reviewer: pick one topic (typically the first or most theologically loaded), write a 2–3 sentence thematic note, and discard the rest.]

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On 'the child grew, and waxed strong in spirit, filled with wisdom: and the grace of God was upon him' (Lk 2:40), Cyril reads the growth-formula as the canonical Lukan anchor of the doctrine of Christ's true humanity: the Word who is eternally complete deliberately assumes the developmental rhythm of human infancy, growing in body and (in his human nature) in the experiential outworking of his hypostatically-united wisdom. Cyril's reading is anti-Apollinarian: Christ has a true human soul that grows. The Tewahedo Miaphysite Christology preserves this doctrine — Christ's growth is real growth in his humanity, not theatrical pretense.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

The twelve-year-old in the Temple says 'I must be about my Father's business,' and Cyril hears in the words the first public utterance of the Son's consciousness of his own divine sonship. The very child who is subject to his earthly parents declares without violation of that subjection that the Father in heaven is his proper Father. Tewahedo Christology cites Lk 2:49 as one of its earliest canonical anchors of the two-natures-in-one-person confession that Cyril would later make definitive against Nestorius.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On 'Jesus increased in wisdom and stature, and in favour with God and man' (Lk 2:52), Cyril reads the fourfold growth-formula (wisdom + stature + favour-with-God + favour-with-man) as the canonical Lukan anti-Apollinarian anchor: the Word does not pretend to grow but truly progresses in the integrated dimensions of his assumed humanity. Cyril's exegesis preserves both the eternal completeness of the Word and the genuine developmental finitude of the assumed nature without confusion. Tewahedo Miaphysite Christology cites Lk 2:52 as the canonical doubled-witness with Lk 2:40 against any Christology that denies Christ's true human soul-growth.

◇ Topic. Topics. This verse appears under: JUDAS, PREACHING, REPENTANCE, CONSEQUENCES OF. [Reviewer: pick one topic (typically the first or most theologically loaded), write a 2–3 sentence thematic note, and discard the rest.]

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On John's 'preaching the baptism of repentance for the remission of sins' (Lk 3:3), Cyril reads the formula as the canonical Lukan anchor that distinguishes John's baptism (signum of repentance) from Christian baptism (sacramentum of regeneration). John's baptism prepares; Christian baptism effects. The Greek aphesin hamartiōn (remission of sins) is the same phrase the Lord uses at the Last Supper (Mt 26:28); John's preaching points forward to the blood-of-the-new-covenant by which the remission becomes effective. Tewahedo baptismal theology cites Lk 3:3 + Mt 26:28 as the canonical doubled-witness that JBaptist's call-to-repentance is the necessary preparation for the effective sacrament of baptismal regeneration.

◇ Topic. Topics. This verse appears under: BAPTISM, FIRE, HUMILITY, JESUS, SHOK. [Reviewer: pick one topic (typically the first or most theologically loaded), write a 2–3 sentence thematic note, and discard the rest.]

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

John's 'He shall baptize you with the Holy Ghost and with fire' is for Cyril the canonical disclosure that Christian baptism is fundamentally pneumatological — the water is the outer sign of an inner gift of the Spirit. The fire is not destruction but the purifying-and-illuminating fire of the Pentecostal descent. Tewahedo baptismal theology reads Lk 3:16 alongside Acts 2 as the canonical diptych of the Spirit's outpouring in the New Covenant.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On 'the Holy Ghost descended in a bodily shape like a dove upon him' (Lk 3:22), Cyril reads Luke's distinctive 'bodily shape' (sōmatikō eidei) as the canonical anchor of the doctrine that the Spirit's descent at the Jordan is not symbolic appearance but a real visible epiphany: the Father, Son, and Spirit are simultaneously and visibly manifested to the Baptist (cf. Jn 1:33-34). Tewahedo Timqät (Theophany) liturgy cites Lk 3:22 as the canonical anchor of the doctrine that the Jordan baptism is THE Trinitarian-epiphany feast — Christ in the water, the Spirit upon, the Father's voice. The Tewahedo annual Timqät procession with the tabot to the water re-enacts the canonical Lukan formula.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On 'Jesus himself began to be about thirty years of age' (Lk 3:23), Cyril reads the precise age-marker at the start of Christ's public ministry as the canonical Lukan anchor of the doctrine that Christ's public ministry begins at the priestly-eligibility age of Levitical service (Num 4:3 — Levites enter active service at thirty). The chronological precision is theological: the true high-priest begins his ministry at the canonical age. Tewahedo episcopal-canon-law preserves a related minimum-age tradition for episcopal consecration, cited as the apostolic-Mosaic warrant for thirty-year-old priestly mature service.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On 'Jesus being full of the Holy Ghost returned from Jordan, and was led by the Spirit into the wilderness' (Lk 4:1), Cyril reads the doubled-pneumatology (full-of-Spirit + led-by-Spirit) as the canonical Lukan anchor of the doctrine that the Spirit-anointing at the Jordan flows immediately into the public ministry's testing: there is no gap between empowerment and trial. Cyril preserves the parallel pattern in apostolic-Christian life: Spirit-baptism (sacramental Confirmation) precedes-but-does-not-prevent the wilderness of testing. Tewahedo monastic-formation cites Lk 4:1 as the canonical anchor that the Spirit who fills also leads-into-wilderness; the bahǝtawi tradition is the appropriation of this Lukan pattern.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

Christ's reply to the devil — 'Thou shalt worship the Lord thy God, and him only shalt thou serve' — is for Cyril the canonical Adamic reversal: where the first Adam consented to the serpent's offered elevation, the New Adam refuses every offered kingdom that does not come through the cross. The temptation is the wilderness-pendant of the Eden episode. Tewahedo Lenten lectionary places the Temptation narrative at the gateway of the Great Fast for precisely this Cyrillian reading.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On Christ's Nazareth-synagogue reading of Isaiah 61 — 'The Spirit of the Lord is upon me, because he hath anointed me to preach the gospel to the poor' (Lk 4:18) — Cyril reads the Christ's-self-identification-with-the-Isaian-Servant as the canonical Lukan anchor of the doctrine of Christ as the Anointed-One (Messiah = Christos): the same Spirit who descended visibly at the Jordan is the Spirit who has been upon Christ from his conception (Lk 1:35), now publicly proclaimed at the inauguration of his ministry. Tewahedo christology cites Lk 4:18 + Is 61:1 as the canonical doubled-witness that the Messianic anointing is pneumatological in essence — the Christ is the Spirit-anointed one whom the prophets foretold.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

After reading from Isaiah, Christ declares 'This day is this scripture fulfilled in your ears.' Cyril reads the synagogue homily as the canonical announcement that the Anointed-One's program is the program of Is 61: good tidings to the poor, release to the captives, sight to the blind, acceptable year. Tewahedo public-reading lectionary still pairs Isaiah 61 with Luke 4 on Nazareth-synagogue feast days to preserve this Cyrillian fulfilment-arc.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On the Capernaum demoniac's involuntary confession 'I know thee who thou art; the Holy One of God' (Lk 4:34), Cyril reads the demon-recognition as the canonical Lukan anchor of the doctrine that the demons see through the veil of the flesh to the divinity of the Word (cf. James 2:19 — 'the devils also believe, and tremble'). The demons' knowledge is true but joyless, contrasted with the human disciples' progressive faith. Tewahedo exorcism-rite (preserved in the Mäshafä Wǝddase Maryam supplications) cites Lk 4:34 as the canonical anchor of the doctrine that the Holy Name itself drives out demons; the priest invokes Christ-the-Holy-One in the formulas of the rite.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

Peter's response to the miraculous catch — 'Depart from me, for I am a sinful man, O Lord' — is for Cyril the first canonical confession of Christ's divinity by the future chief apostle: only divine power could so command the deep, and only divine holiness compels such a confession of unworthiness. Cyril notes that Christ does not depart but rather summons Peter from sinfulness to apostleship. Tewahedo ordination liturgy preserves the Petrine 'depart from me' as the canonical posture of the one called to priesthood.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On Christ's commission to Simon 'Fear not; from henceforth thou shalt catch men' (Lk 5:10), Cyril reads the fishers-of-men image as the canonical Lukan anchor of the apostolic-vocation: Peter who failed at fishing all night succeeds at men-fishing under Christ's word, foreshadowing the apostolic catch of three-thousand on the Day of Pentecost (Acts 2:41). The verb zōgrōn (capture-alive) is positive: the apostolic mission rescues, does not destroy. Tewahedo apostolic theology cites Lk 5:10 + Acts 2:41 as the canonical doubled-witness that the apostolic mission is the bringing-into-life of those caught by the gospel; the Tewahedo episcopate continues the apostolic catch.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On Christ's 'putting forth his hand, [he] touched him, saying, I will: be thou clean' (Lk 5:13), Cyril reads the touch-of-the-leper as the canonical Lukan anchor of the doctrine that the Incarnation reverses the contagion-pattern of impurity: the leper does NOT defile Christ, Christ cleanses the leper. Christ's bodily-touch is sacramental — the Word-made-flesh transfers life and cleanness by physical contact. Tewahedo healing-rite theology cites Lk 5:13 as the canonical anchor for the bodily-touch component of the laying-on-of-hands healing rite and for the priestly anointing-with-oil (cf. James 5:14); the priest's hand carries Christ's cleansing.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

When Christ tells the paralytic 'Man, thy sins are forgiven thee,' Cyril hears the canonical claim of divine prerogative — only God forgives sins, as the scribes themselves know. The subsequent healing of the body is offered as the visible sign of the invisible remission. Cyril emphasizes the order: sins forgiven first, then body restored, so that the priority of soul over body is canonically established. Tewahedo penitential theology cites Lk 5:20-24 as the principal Lukan anchor of sacramental absolution.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On Christ's 'But that ye may know that the Son of man hath power upon earth to forgive sins' (Lk 5:24), Cyril reads the title 'Son of Man' coupled with divine-prerogative-to-forgive as the canonical Lukan anchor of the Christological argument that runs through the Synoptics: only God forgives sins (so the scribes correctly observe at Mk 2:7), the Son of Man forgives sins, therefore the Son of Man is divine. The miracle of healing the paralytic is the sign confirming the antecedent claim. Tewahedo absolution-theology cites Lk 5:24 as the canonical anchor for the priestly absolution formula — the priest forgives in persona Christi, exercising the authority Christ gave the apostles (Jn 20:23).

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On Christ's 'I came not to call the righteous, but sinners to repentance' (Lk 5:32), Cyril reads the mission-statement as the canonical Lukan anchor of the doctrine that the Incarnation is medicinal-soteriological in character: Christ-the-Physician comes for the sick. The pairing with Lk 19:10 ('the Son of man is come to seek and to save that which was lost') frames the entire Lukan trajectory as a search-and-rescue mission. Tewahedo penance-theology cites Lk 5:32 as the canonical anchor of the doctrine that the priest's call-to-confession follows the dominical pattern: not exclusion of sinners but invitation to repentance.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

'The Son of Man is Lord also of the sabbath' — Cyril reads the declaration as the canonical assertion of Christ's divinity by way of his lordship over the Mosaic institution. The same Word who instituted the Sabbath in creation and re-instituted it at Sinai stands as its Lord. Tewahedo Saturday-Sabbath theology cites Lk 6:5 as the canonical warrant that the Lord of the Sabbath is precisely the One who fulfills, not abrogates, its rest in himself.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On Christ's choice of the Twelve 'whom also he named apostles' (Lk 6:13), Cyril reads the act-of-naming-twelve as the canonical Lukan anchor of the doctrine of apostolic foundation: the twelve are not crowd-volunteers but Christ-selected after a night of prayer (Lk 6:12). The number twelve corresponds typologically to the twelve patriarchs/tribes; the new Israel is constituted with apostolic foundation. Tewahedo episcopal theology cites Lk 6:13 as the canonical Lukan anchor of the apostolic origin of the episcopate — the bishop's authority traces by laying-on-of-hands through unbroken succession to the apostolic Twelve.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

The Lukan Beatitudes — 'Blessed are ye poor, for yours is the kingdom of God' — are for Cyril the canonical pendant to the Matthean form: where Matthew preserves the inner-disposition reading (poor in spirit), Luke preserves the outer-condition reading (literally poor). Cyril harmonizes the two as a single canonical blessing of those whose poverty — whether of possession or of spirit — opens them to the kingdom. Tewahedo monastic catechesis draws explicitly on the Lukan form.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On Christ's 'Love your enemies, do good to them which hate you' (Lk 6:27), Cyril reads the love-your-enemies command as the canonical Lukan anchor of the doctrine that Christian ethics transcends Old-Testament-reciprocity: the love-of-enemies is impossible without participation in the Father's own love (Mt 5:45 — 'he maketh his sun to rise on the evil and on the good'). The command is not natural-virtue but supernatural-grace. Tewahedo monastic ethics cites Lk 6:27 + Mt 5:44-45 as the canonical doubled-witness for the bahǝtawi's practice of love-of-enemies — the desert prayer 'forgive my enemies as Thou hast forgiven me' is the appropriation of this command.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On the Lukan Golden Rule 'And as ye would that men should do to you, do ye also to them likewise' (Lk 6:31), Cyril reads the positive-formulation (do-as-you-would) as the canonical Lukan anchor of the doctrine that Christian ethics is active-positive, not merely abstinence-from-evil. Where Hillel's negative Golden Rule said 'what is hateful to you, do not do to others,' the dominical positive Golden Rule commands proactive good. Tewahedo catechesis cites Lk 6:31 as the canonical Lukan anchor for the active-positive structure of Christian charity preserved in the seven works of mercy taught in the Mäshafä Sǝʾatat (Book of Hours) catechetical material.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On Christ's 'Be ye therefore merciful, as your Father also is merciful' (Lk 6:36), Cyril reads the Lukan be-merciful command as the distinctive Lukan parallel to Matthew's 'Be ye therefore perfect, as your Father which is in heaven is perfect' (Mt 5:48) — Luke's choice of 'merciful' (oiktirmones) rather than 'perfect' (teleios) highlights the Father's mercy as the defining attribute that Christian ethics imitates. Tewahedo soteriology cites Lk 6:36 + Mt 5:48 as the canonical doubled-witness for the theosis-pattern: Christian perfection IS divine mercy received and extended.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

When Christ marvels at the centurion — 'I have not found so great faith, no, not in Israel' — Cyril reads the praise as the canonical anticipation of the Gentile mission. The Gentile believes without having seen the temple; the Israelite, having the temple before his eyes, often does not. Cyril treats Lk 7:9 as the Lukan precursor of his beloved Pauline 'there is neither Jew nor Greek' (Gal 3:28). Tewahedo missiology cites the centurion as a canonical Gentile-faith anchor.

◇ Topic. Topics. This verse appears under: BURGLARY, DEAD. [Reviewer: pick one topic (typically the first or most theologically loaded), write a 2–3 sentence thematic note, and discard the rest.]

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On the raising of the Nain widow's son — 'and there came a fear on all: and they glorified God, saying, That a great prophet is risen up among us' (Lk 7:12, 16) — Cyril reads the first resurrection-miracle in Luke as the canonical Lukan anchor of Christ's master-over-death authority, prefiguring the Lazarus raising (Jn 11) and ultimately Christ's own resurrection. The Nain widow's son is the third raising-from-the-dead in scripture (after Elijah at Zarephath and Elisha at Shunem); Christ's act surpasses the prophets — he raises without prayer, by his own word. Tewahedo funeral office cites Lk 7:12 as the canonical Lukan anchor of the doctrine that Christ's compassion at the bier of the widow's son is the model for priestly compassion at every funeral.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On Christ's answer to JBaptist's disciples 'the blind see, the lame walk, the lepers are cleansed, the deaf hear, the dead are raised, to the poor the gospel is preached' (Lk 7:22), Cyril reads the sixfold sign-list as the canonical Lukan anchor of the doctrine that Christ's identity is demonstrated by the Isaian Messianic signs (Is 35:5-6 + Is 61:1) — the same prophet whom John quoted at his preaching now points back to him. The signs are cumulative-eschatological: the Messianic age has arrived. Tewahedo christology cites Lk 7:22 + Is 35 + Is 61 as the canonical triple-witness of the prophetic-fulfillment evidence of Christ's identity.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On the title 'The Son of man is come eating and drinking; and ye say, Behold a gluttonous man, and a winebibber' (Lk 7:34), Cyril reads the eating-and-drinking-Christology as the canonical Lukan anchor of the doctrine that the Word's true humanity includes ordinary table-fellowship: Christ does not impose monastic asceticism on his ministry (unlike John). The dominical pattern (Luke especially) is the table-as-pulpit. Tewahedo monastic theology preserves the doubled-witness: the monastic ascetic follows John's pattern, but the parish priest follows Christ's table-fellowship pattern; both are canonical.

◇ Topic. Topics. This verse appears under: POWER OF, LOVE, SALVATION. [Reviewer: pick one topic (typically the first or most theologically loaded), write a 2–3 sentence thematic note, and discard the rest.]

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

'Her sins, which are many, are forgiven; for she loved much' — Cyril reads the saying not as forgiveness CAUSED by love but as forgiveness MANIFESTED by love. The woman's tears at the Pharisee's table are the visible token that her sins are already remitted; the love comes from the remission, the remission is not the wage of the love. Tewahedo penitential rite preserves this Cyrillian ordering: absolution precedes the works of penance, never the reverse.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On Christ's direct absolution-formula 'And he said unto her, Thy sins are forgiven' (Lk 7:48), Cyril reads the second-person performative declaration (apheōntai sou hai hamartiai) as the canonical Lukan anchor of the priestly absolution-formula: Christ-in-Person speaks forgiveness directly, paralleled by the Johannine commission to the apostles (Jn 20:23 — 'Whose soever sins ye remit, they are remitted unto them'). Tewahedo penance-theology cites Lk 7:48 + Jn 20:23 as the canonical doubled-witness for the priestly absolution: the priest declares 'thy sins are forgiven' in persona Christi, exercising the apostolic commission.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On Christ's interpretation of the Sower parable 'Now the parable is this: The seed is the word of God' (Lk 8:11), Cyril reads the seed = word-of-God identification as the canonical Lukan anchor of the doctrine of the dynamic Word: the Word that creates (Gen 1) is the same Word that saves (the Gospel) and the same Word that perseveres (the canonical Scripture). The Word is seed because it carries its own life within itself. Tewahedo Bible-doctrine cites Lk 8:11 as the canonical Lukan anchor for the doctrine of Scripture-as-living-seed; the Tewahedo manuscript tradition's reverence for the canonical word as bearing-its-own-life is rooted here.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

'Where is your faith?' — Christ's rebuke to the terrified disciples in the storm is for Cyril the canonical anchor of the disciples' still-developing recognition of his divinity. The Word who rebukes the wind is the same Word who, in Job and the Psalms, treads upon the waves of the sea. The storm-stilling is a theophanic act, and the disciples' question 'who then is this?' answers itself. Tewahedo Christology cites Lk 8:25 as a canonical anchor of the disciples' graduated confession of the Word's divinity.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On the description of the freed Gerasene demoniac '[they] found the man, out of whom the devils were departed, sitting at the feet of Jesus, clothed, and in his right mind' (Lk 8:35), Cyril reads the threefold-restoration (sitting-at-feet + clothed + right-mind) as the canonical Lukan anchor of the doctrine of Christian sanity as the restoration of catechetical posture: the freed soul takes its rightful disciple-position at the feet of the Lord, with the moral garments of virtue and the rational integration of right-mindedness. Tewahedo catechumenate cites Lk 8:35 as the canonical anchor for the catechumen's restored posture — at Christ's feet, clothed in the white baptismal garment, sound of mind.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On Christ's 'Daughter, be of good comfort: thy faith hath made thee whole; go in peace' (Lk 8:48), Cyril reads the second instance of the Lukan 'thy-faith-hath-saved-thee' formula (after Lk 7:50) as the canonical Lukan anchor of the doctrine that the woman's bold-touch-in-faith is the active-receptive cause of her healing: she did not need permission, the touch of faith drew Christ's power. Tewahedo theology of bold-faith cites Lk 8:48 as the canonical anchor of the doctrine that the believing approach to the consecrated species (the body and blood) is the parallel of the hemorrhaging woman's healing-touch; one approaches the Eucharist in the same posture.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On Christ's call to Jairus's daughter 'Maid, arise' (Lk 8:54), Cyril reads the imperative-command-as-resurrection-word as the canonical Lukan anchor of the doctrine that Christ's word effects what it says: the same authority that 'in the beginning was the Word' (Jn 1:1) speaks the dead to life with a single imperative. The doubled-raising of Lk 7 (Nain) + Lk 8 (Jairus's daughter) prepares the reader for Lk 24 (Christ's own resurrection): Christ raises others by his word, then is raised himself by the Father. Tewahedo Fasika theology cites Lk 8:54 + Jn 11:43 + Lk 24:46 as the canonical triple-witness of the word-effects-resurrection power that culminates in Christ's own raising.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

Peter's 'Thou art the Christ of God' is for Cyril the apostolic confession that defines the Church. The confession is not Peter's discovery — it is the Father's revelation through him — and yet it is Peter's voice that utters it on behalf of the Twelve. Cyril treats the Lukan form alongside the Matthean expansion (Mt 16:16-18) as a single canonical confession-and-commission diptych. Tewahedo Petrine theology cites Lk 9:20 as the canonical anchor of the apostolic confessional core.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On Christ's 'If any man will come after me, let him deny himself, and take up his cross DAILY, and follow me' (Lk 9:23), Cyril reads the distinctive Lukan 'daily' (kath' hēmeran) as the canonical Lukan anchor of the doctrine that Christian discipleship is not a one-time martyrdom but a daily continuing renunciation: the cross is taken up every morning, not once at conversion. Tewahedo monastic theology cites Lk 9:23 + the kath'-hēmeran daily-renewal pattern as the canonical anchor of the monastic morning-prayer renunciation: each day the bahǝtawi renews his vows, takes up his daily cross. The daily-eucharist tradition in some Tewahedo monasteries draws on the same Lukan pattern of daily Christ-following.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On Luke's distinctive note about the Transfiguration conversation — '[Moses and Elias] spake of his decease (exodon) which he should accomplish at Jerusalem' (Lk 9:31) — Cyril reads the Greek exodon as the canonical Lukan double-entendre: Christ's death is his Exodus from this world AND the New-Exodus that liberates humanity from sin's bondage (the Moses-Exodus parallel made explicit). Moses, who led the first Exodus, now consults with the Greater-Moses who is about to lead the second. Tewahedo Buhe (Transfiguration) theology cites Lk 9:31 as the canonical Lukan anchor of the doctrine that the Transfiguration is the apocalypse of the coming Pascha — the same glory will return at the Resurrection.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

The voice from the cloud at the Transfiguration — 'This is my beloved Son: hear him' — is for Cyril the canonical Father-Son disclosure made visible to Peter, James, and John. The Transfiguration is the proleptic glimpse of the post-Resurrection glory: the same flesh that walks the wilderness shines with the uncreated light. Tewahedo Transfiguration feast (Buhe) preserves Cyril's reading of the divine voice as the canonical paternal attestation of the Son's inseparable divinity.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On the Lukan turning-point 'when the time was come that he should be received up, he stedfastly set his face to go to Jerusalem' (Lk 9:51), Cyril reads the set-his-face formula (estērisen to prosōpon — Hebraism: 'set the face') as the canonical Lukan anchor of the doctrine of Christ's deliberate-voluntary Passion: the Lord is not seized by hostile forces, he moves toward Jerusalem with intentional resolve. The verb 'received up' (analēmpseōs) embraces death + resurrection + ascension as a single integrated movement. Tewahedo theology of the voluntary Passion cites Lk 9:51 as the canonical Lukan anchor for the doctrine that Christ goes willingly to Calvary — the Lent-and-Holy-Week observance follows the Lukan travel-narrative geography.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On Christ's appointing 'other seventy [or seventy-two] also, and sent them two and two before his face' (Lk 10:1), Cyril reads the seventy(-two)-disciples as the canonical Lukan anchor of the ministry-beyond-the-apostolic-Twelve: the wider missionary commission corresponds typologically to Moses' seventy elders (Num 11:24-25) and (in the longer reading) to the seventy-two nations of the Table of Nations (Gen 10 LXX). The two-by-two pattern preserves the Deuteronomic two-witness rule (Deu 19:15) — apostolic witness is plural and corroborated. Tewahedo missionary theology cites Lk 10:1 + Num 11:24 + Deu 19:15 as the canonical triple-witness for the wider-than-apostolic missionary commission and the two-witness norm of evangelization.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On Christ's 'I beheld Satan as lightning fall from heaven' (Lk 10:18), Cyril reads the seventy-two's return-with-joy + Christ's prophetic-vision as the canonical Lukan anchor of the doctrine that the apostolic-missionary expansion enacts in time the eternally-decided defeat of Satan. The lightning-image draws on Is 14:12 (Lucifer fall) and is parallel to Rev 12:7-9. Tewahedo angelology, drawing on 1 Enoch 86 (Mäṣḥafä Hēnok's account of fallen stars), cites Lk 10:18 + 1 En 86 + Rev 12:7-9 as the canonical triple-witness of Satan's heavenly-fall — the same fall is the eschatological referent of every successful exorcism in apostolic mission.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On the Lukan 'In that hour Jesus rejoiced in spirit, and said, I thank thee, O Father, Lord of heaven and earth' (Lk 10:21), Cyril reads the doubly-emphatic 'rejoiced in spirit' (or, in the alternative reading, 'rejoiced in the Holy Spirit') as the canonical Lukan anchor of an explicit Trinitarian utterance in the public ministry: the Son addresses the Father in the joy of the Holy Spirit, voicing in time the eternal perichoresis of the divine persons. The verse is among the most-cited Lukan Trinitarian-cantilenas in Tewahedo Liturgy of the Hours. Tewahedo Trinitarian-doxology cites Lk 10:21 + Mt 28:19 + 2 Cor 13:14 as the canonical Lukan-NT triple-witness of explicit Trinitarian-prayer.

◇ Athanasius of Alexandria (350). Athanasius of Alexandria Orationes contra Arianos III.46 (c. 350 AD)

'No man knoweth who the Son is, but the Father; and who the Father is, but the Son, and he to whom the Son will reveal him' — Athanasius marks Lk 10:22 as the LUKAN-PARALLEL of Mt 11:27 (treated already in γ.4.9 seed) — the MUTUAL-KNOWLEDGE-FORMULA as homoousion-prooftext. The Lukan-form's variation (oudeis ginōskei tis estin ho huios ei mē ho Patēr — 'no one knoweth WHO the Son is') emphasizes the WHO-question: the Son's-identity is known exhaustively-only-by-the-Father, and reciprocally. Only-equals-by-nature exhaustively-know-each-other (a creature could not exhaustively-know the Father). The exhaustive-mutual-knowledge therefore-requires homoousion. Pairs with Mt 11:27 as the canonical-synoptic mutual-knowledge-homoousion pair.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On the lawyer's recital 'Thou shalt love the Lord thy God with all thy heart, and with all thy soul, and with all thy strength, and with all thy mind; and thy neighbour as thyself' (Lk 10:27), Cyril reads the dominical-approved double-summary as the canonical Lukan anchor of the Greatest Commandment in its FOURFOLD heart/soul/strength/mind form (Luke alone among the Synoptics expands 'might' to 'strength + mind'). The four-faculty totality is taken up by the Tewahedo catechumenate as the framework for the four exercises of faith: heart (prayer) + soul (worship) + strength (ascetic labour) + mind (catechetical study). Tewahedo monastic spirituality cites Lk 10:27 + Deu 6:5 as the canonical fourfold-doubled-witness for the integral-formation pattern of the bahǝtawi.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

Cyril reads the Good Samaritan parable as a Christological allegory from at least the third Christian century, and his exegesis carries the canonical pattern: the wounded man is Adam-fallen-among-thieves; the priest and Levite are Law and Prophets, unable to save; the Samaritan is the despised-and-rejected Christ; the inn is the Church; the two denarii are the Old and New Testaments; the promise to return is the Second Coming. Tewahedo monastic exegesis preserves this allegory wholesale.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

The Lukan Pater Noster — 'When ye pray, say, Our Father which art in heaven' — is for Cyril the canonical form by which the Word teaches the saints to address God in his own filial voice. The 'Our' is ecclesial: no one prays the prayer alone; even the hermit in the desert prays as a member of the body. Tewahedo daily-office incorporates the Pater Noster at every hour, drawing directly on Cyril's reading of it as the canonical-filial address form.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On the Lukan Lord's-Prayer petition 'And forgive us our sins; for we also forgive every one that is indebted to us' (Lk 11:4), Cyril reads the forgive-as-forgiven-by-us clause as the canonical Lukan anchor of the doctrine that human forgiveness of debtors is the operative condition (not the meritorious cause) of receiving divine forgiveness. The clause's reciprocal grammar makes the Lord's Prayer the only canonical petitionary text that requires the petitioner to take an ethical action FIRST. Tewahedo penance-theology cites Lk 11:4 + Mt 6:12 + Mt 6:14-15 as the canonical triple-witness that priestly absolution presupposes the penitent's forgiveness of debtors; sacramental confession is incomplete without horizontal reconciliation.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On Christ's 'how much more shall your heavenly Father give the Holy Spirit to them that ask him' (Lk 11:13), Cyril reads the distinctive-Lukan substitution of 'Holy Spirit' (where Matthew has 'good things' at Mt 7:11) as the canonical Lukan anchor of the doctrine that the supreme petition of Christian prayer is the gift of the Spirit himself, not merely material or providential goods. Luke's pneumatological theology is integrated throughout: the Spirit is the Promise-of-the-Father (Lk 24:49 + Acts 1:4-5) and the gift to which all other gifts are subordinate. Tewahedo charismatic-prayer-theology cites Lk 11:13 as the canonical Lukan anchor for the doctrine that the Holy Spirit is the answer to every prayer 'in Jesus' name'.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On Christ's 'But if I with the finger of God cast out devils' (Lk 11:20), Cyril reads the distinctive-Lukan 'finger of God' (where Mt 12:28 has 'Spirit of God') as the canonical Lukan anchor that links Christ's exorcisms to the Exodus plague-narratives — the Egyptian magicians confessed 'this is the finger of God' (Exo 8:19). The Mosaic 'finger' = the Spirit, the same Spirit that wrote the Decalogue on stone tablets (Exo 31:18). Tewahedo christology cites Lk 11:20 + Exo 8:19 + Exo 31:18 + Mt 12:28 as the canonical fourfold-witness that the Mosaic-finger-of-God is identified with the Spirit-of-God operative in Christ's ministry; the same Spirit is the divine agent of liberation and of canon-formation.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On the unnamed woman's exclamation 'Blessed is the womb that bare thee, and the paps which thou hast sucked' (Lk 11:27), Cyril reads the public Marian beatitude — uniquely Lukan among the Synoptics — as the canonical Lukan-public-anchor of the Theotokos doctrine: even mid-public-ministry, a Galilean crowd-woman utters spontaneous Marian-praise. Christ's response 'yea rather, blessed are they that hear the word of God, and keep it' does NOT cancel her praise but transfers its principle: Mary's blessedness is not biological-only but also the FIRST exemplar of hearing-and-keeping (Lk 1:38). Tewahedo Wǝddase Maryam preserves Lk 11:27 alongside Lk 1:42 as the canonical doubled-Lukan public-Marian-beatitudes.

◇ Topic. Topics. This verse appears under: HOLY SPIRIT, PENITENT, ING TO, CONSEQUENCES OF, UNPARDONABLE SIN. [Reviewer: pick one topic (typically the first or most theologically loaded), write a 2–3 sentence thematic note, and discard the rest.]

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On Christ's 'unto him that blasphemeth against the Holy Ghost it shall not be forgiven' (Lk 12:10), Cyril reads the unpardonable-sin clause as the canonical Lukan anchor of the doctrine that the sin against the Spirit is not a single verbal lapse but a settled-final refusal of the Spirit's offered forgiveness: one who rejects the agent of forgiveness cannot be forgiven. The unpardonability is structural-receptive, not capricious. Tewahedo penance-theology cites Lk 12:10 + Mk 3:29 as the canonical doubled-witness that the Spirit is the active-agent of every absolution; willful-final refusal of the Spirit closes the door from the human side. Pastoral wisdom in the Tewahedo confessional tradition: anyone WORRIED about having committed this sin has not committed it.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

'Thou fool, this night thy soul shall be required of thee' — the rich fool's death-warrant in the parable is for Cyril the canonical warning against the postponement-of-the-soul that worldly wealth enables. The man addresses HIS OWN SOUL ('soul, thou hast much goods') as if it belonged to him to dispose; God's reply discloses that the soul is on lease. Tewahedo monastic memento-mori catechesis draws directly on this Cyrillian reading.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On Christ's 'Fear not, little flock; for it is your Father's good pleasure to give you the kingdom' (Lk 12:32), Cyril reads the doubled-tenderness (little flock + Father's good pleasure) as the canonical Lukan anchor of the doctrine that the Church's smallness is no obstacle to the Kingdom's gift: God's good pleasure (eudokia — the same word as 'good will toward men' in the angelic hymn Lk 2:14) does not measure recipients by their numbers. Tewahedo ecclesiology, mindful of the minority-position of the Ethiopian Tewahedo Church in the broader Christian world, cites Lk 12:32 as the canonical anchor of the doctrine that the small flock of right-believers (the Tewahedo 'right-believing' identity) receives the same kingdom as any larger communion.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On Christ's 'I have a baptism to be baptized with; and how am I straitened till it be accomplished!' (Lk 12:50), Cyril reads the future-passive-baptism (paraphrasably 'I am-to-be-baptized') as the canonical Lukan anchor of the doctrine that Christ's death-and-resurrection is itself the baptism-toward-which-his-earthly-baptism-pointed: the Jordan baptism prefigures, the Cross effects. The straitening-cry expresses the Word-in-flesh's deep human anticipation of the Passion. Tewahedo baptismal theology cites Lk 12:50 + Rom 6:3-4 ('baptized into his death') as the canonical doubled-witness that Christian baptism participates in Christ's own death-baptism; the catechumen is buried with Christ in the font.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On the parable of the barren fig tree — 'Behold, these three years I come seeking fruit on this fig tree, and find none: cut it down' (Lk 13:7) — Cyril reads the three-years-fruit-seeking + intercessor's-plea-for-one-more-year as the canonical Lukan anchor of the doctrine of patient divine longsuffering: God seeks fruit, the Son intercedes for delay, the Spirit's manure-and-digging (the means of grace) is given for one more year. The three years correspond to the public ministry; the digging-and-dunging is the apostolic-and-monastic intercession. Tewahedo intercessory theology cites Lk 13:7 as the canonical Lukan anchor of the doctrine that priestly intercession can postpone (though not forever) the just judgment of fruitlessness.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

'This woman, being a daughter of Abraham, whom Satan hath bound, lo, these eighteen years, ought not she to be loosed from this bond on the sabbath day?' — Cyril reads Christ's defence of the Sabbath healing as the canonical exposure of the Pharisaic perversion of the rest-commandment into a bondage. The Sabbath was made for the loosing of bonds, not for their maintenance. Tewahedo Sabbatical theology cites Lk 13:16 as the canonical anchor of the freedom-form of Sabbath rest.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On Christ's 'Strive to enter in at the strait gate: for many, I say unto you, will seek to enter in, and shall not be able' (Lk 13:24), Cyril reads the strive (agōnizesthe) + many-shall-not-be-able as the canonical Lukan anchor of the doctrine that salvation requires sustained-effort, not merely benign assent: the verb (root of 'agony') is the same Paul uses for the apostolic struggle (1 Cor 9:25, 1 Tim 6:12). The 'many shall not be able' warns against presumption. Tewahedo monastic theology cites Lk 13:24 + 1 Cor 9:25 as the canonical doubled-witness for the strenuous-character of Christian discipleship; the desert-fathers' ascetic 'agon' against the demons is the appropriation of this command.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On Christ's 'they shall come from the east, and from the west, and from the north, and from the south, and shall sit down in the kingdom of God' (Lk 13:29), Cyril reads the fourfold-compass-points eschatological-gathering as the canonical Lukan anchor of the doctrine of the universal-Gentile inclusion in the eschatological-banquet: salvation flows from the East (where Cyril sees the dominant Mediterranean diaspora) but also the West, the North, and the South — explicitly including Ethiopia. Tewahedo missionary-eschatology cites Lk 13:29 as the canonical Lukan anchor for the doctrine that the southern-African (specifically Ethiopian) inclusion in the eschatological-Kingdom is canonically promised — the Acts 8 Ethiopian eunuch is the firstfruits of this fulfilment.

The phrase recurs in Luke at 18:14 (Pharisee + Publican), making it a Lukan refrain. Tewahedo monastic spirituality cites Lk 14:11 + Lk 18:14 + Phil 2:8-9 (Christ's-own humiliation-and-exaltation) as the canonical triple-witness of the humility-precedes-exaltation pattern — the same pattern Christ embodies in the Incarnation.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On the parable of the Great Supper — 'A certain man made a great supper, and bade many' (Lk 14:16) — Cyril reads the supper-parable as the canonical Lukan anchor of the Eucharistic-eschatological-banquet image: the great supper is the messianic feast (anticipated at Lk 13:29 from-east-and-west) prepared by the Father, hosted by the Son, distributed by the Spirit's apostolic invitations. The original-invited (covenant-elect) excuse themselves; the streets-and-lanes poor are brought in. Tewahedo Eucharistic theology cites Lk 14:16 alongside the Marriage Supper of the Lamb (Rev 19:9) as the canonical Lukan-Apocalyptic doubled-witness of the Eucharist as the present-eschatological-banquet: every Mass anticipates the Great Supper.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On the master's command 'Go out into the highways and hedges, and compel them to come in' (Lk 14:23), Cyril reads the compel-them-to-come-in (anagkason eiselthein) as the canonical Lukan anchor of the doctrine of evangelistic-persuasive-urgency: the apostolic invitation is not coy permission but earnest-pressing constraint. Cyril is emphatic that the constraint is moral-persuasive, never physical-coercive (Augustine's later use of this verse to justify coercion against the Donatists is not Cyril's reading). Tewahedo missionary-pastoral theology cites Lk 14:23 as the canonical Lukan anchor of the doctrine that evangelistic urgency is genuine pressure of love, not state-religious coercion.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

'Whosoever doth not bear his cross, and come after me, cannot be my disciple' — Cyril reads the saying as the canonical anchor of voluntary martyrdom: the disciple's own cross-bearing is the imitation that conforms him to the Master. The discipleship cost-calculation that follows (tower-builder, king-going-to-war) is the prudential prelude. Tewahedo monastic profession includes the voluntary embrace of the spiritual cross in direct dependence on this Cyrillian reading.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On Christ's 'Whosoever he be of you that forsaketh not all that he hath, he cannot be my disciple' (Lk 14:33), Cyril reads the forsake-all-that-he-hath (panta apotassetai tois huparchousin) as the canonical Lukan anchor of the doctrine of total-discipleship: the cost-of-discipleship parable (counting-the-tower + counting-the-battle) issues in this absolute. Cyril does not read 'forsake' as universal-divestment for every Christian but as the inner-detachment that some are called to embody outwardly in monastic profession. Tewahedo monastic theology cites Lk 14:33 as the canonical Lukan anchor of the bahǝtawi's vow of poverty — the monastic outwardly enacts what every disciple inwardly possesses.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

Mary at Christ's feet 'hath chosen that good part' — Cyril reads the saying as the canonical anchor of contemplative priority: the service to the body (Martha) is necessary but the service to the Word (Mary) is the 'one thing needful,' and Mary's portion will not be taken from her. Tewahedo monastic theology cites Lk 10:42 as one of its earliest canonical anchors of the primacy of prayer and scripture-reading over outward works.

epub:type="footnote">

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On Christ's 'No man, having put his hand to the plough, and looking back, is fit for the kingdom of God' (Lk 9:62), Cyril reads the plough-image as the canonical Lukan anchor of the doctrine that the kingdom requires unbroken forward-orientation: the disciple resembles Elisha (1 Ki 19:19-21) who left the plough to follow Elijah without looking back. The image of the plough turning the soil prefigures the apostolic mission turning the soil of the nations for the seed of the gospel. Tewahedo monastic-formation cites Lk 9:62 + 1 Ki 19:19-21 as the canonical doubled-witness for the irreversibility of monastic profession: once the bahǝtawi has begun, he does not look back.

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On Christ's 'Thy faith hath saved thee; go in peace' (Lk 7:50), Cyril reads the doubled-dismissal (faith-has-saved + go-in-peace) as the canonical Lukan anchor of the doctrine of justifying faith: the sinful woman's faith is the operative cause of her salvation, the peace is the eschatological dismissal-formula. The phrase recurs at Lk 8:48 (Jairus's daughter / hemorrhaging woman) and Lk 17:19 (Samaritan leper) and Lk 18:42 (blind beggar) — four 'thy-faith-hath-saved-thee' formulas in Luke, an emphatic Lukan refrain. Tewahedo dismissal-formula in the Eucharistic anaphora preserves a parallel structure: 'Go in the peace of the Lord.'

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On Christ's 'I must preach the kingdom of God to other cities also: for therefore am I sent' (Lk 4:43), Cyril reads the dei (it-is-necessary) + apestalmai (I-am-sent) as the canonical Lukan anchor of the doctrine of Christ's missional self-understanding: the Word's earthly ministry is mission-bound from above, not crowd-driven from below. The verb apostellō becomes the New-Testament technical-term for divine-sending; Christ's apostolic-mission is the model for the apostolic-mission of the disciples (Jn 20:21 — 'as my Father hath sent me, even so send I you'). Tewahedo missionary theology cites Lk 4:43 + Jn 20:21 as the canonical doubled-witness that the Tewahedo evangelistic mission to the Beta-Israel and surrounding nations participates in Christ's own apostolic-sending.

epub:type="footnote">

◇ Cyril of Alexandria (430). Cyril of Alexandria Commentary on Luke (c. 430 AD)

On the climax of the Lukan genealogy '...which was the son of Adam, which was the son of God' (Lk 3:38), Cyril reads the back-to-Adam-and-God terminus as the canonical Lukan anchor of the doctrine of Christ-as-Second-Adam (1 Cor 15:45) — the Word assumes the humanity of Adam universally, not merely the lineage of Abraham (as in Mt 1's genealogy). Luke's genealogy is theological: Christ is heir of all humanity, the Restorer of the entire Adamic race. Tewahedo soteriology cites Lk 3:38 + 1 Cor 15:45 as the canonical doubled-witness that Christ's redemption is universal-Adamic in scope, not limited to ethnic Israel.

epub:type="footnote">

◇ Athanasius of Alexandria (350). Athanasius of Alexandria Orationes contra Arianos III.51 (c. 350 AD)

'And Jesus increased in wisdom and stature, and in favour with God and man' — Athanasius marks proekopten sophia kai hēlikia (he advanced in wisdom and stature) as a QUA-FLESH developmental-increase, NOT a qua-Word increase-in-wisdom (the Word is the Father's-Wisdom by-essence and cannot become-wiser). The advancement names the assumed-flesh's-real-growth: an infant-Word grows into a young-Word; a young-Word learns the carpenter's-trade; the developmentally-real human-experience is genuinely-experienced by the Word-incarnate without compromising the Word's-eternal-perfection-in-knowledge. Crucial anti-Docetic and anti-Apollinarian text: Christ's-humanity is developmentally-genuine, not pre-formed-or-shortcut. Tewahedo Christology preserves this developmental-realism in its catechesis of the child-Jesus's growth.
